If the proposal delivers targeted flexibility (for example, around certain uses, authorisations, or procedural pathways for defence-related needs), it could:
- reduce time-to-market for defence-adjacent products,
- improve predictability for investors and customers,
- and make it easier for SMEs to qualify as suppliers to primes.

But there’s a flip side: any “defence readiness” carve-outs must still preserve environmental and health protections. SMEs will want clarity on **scope** (what counts as “defence readiness”), **documentation requirements**, and **how long any flexibility lasts**.

### 2) Defence primes (risk management and procurement certainty)
For primes, the biggest pain point is **supply chain fragility**. Even when a prime has a contract, it can struggle if a key input is constrained by chemicals rules. A regulation that better aligns chemical restrictions with defence investment conditions could:
- lower procurement risk,
- support multi-year contracting (because compliance timelines become more manageable),
- and reduce the need for bespoke workarounds that are expensive and slow.

Primes will likely push for **clear eligibility criteria** and **stable regulatory pathways** so that they can plan production capacity and qualification schedules.

### 3) Regulators and oversight bodies (balancing speed with safeguards)
Regulators will face a classic trade-off: **speed versus scrutiny**. The compromise text’s existence suggests the EU is trying to build a mechanism that allows defence-related needs to be met without undermining the core chemicals framework. Expect attention on:
- governance (who decides when defence readiness applies),
- transparency and reporting,
- and safeguards to ensure temporary flexibility doesn’t become a permanent loophole.

**So what? Two smart insights**  
1) **Defence readiness is becoming a regulatory engineering problem, not just a budget problem.** If the EU can reduce compliance bottlenecks for critical inputs, it can unlock investment without necessarily increasing headline funding. That’s a quieter but potentially high-impact lever.  
2) **The “chemicals-to-capital” link is tightening.** Investors and lenders increasingly price regulatory uncertainty. If the EU clarifies defence-related conditions under REACH/CLP/biocides/POPs/PFAS frameworks, it can improve bankability for suppliers—especially SMEs that struggle most with compliance costs.

Bron: `eur-lex.europa.eu` · Datum: 2026-04-20T05:35:00.418948+00:00  
Programma: Other/NA  
## Market abuse meets procurement reality: Court clarifies “inside information” when public contracts are in play

**What happened (and why it matters)**  
On **16 April 2026**, the Court of Justice of the EU (Fourth Chamber) delivered its judgment in **Case C‑229/24 (TK and OP v Riksåklagaren)**. The case is a **preliminary ruling** from Sweden’s Supreme Court (Högsta domstolen) on how to interpret **Regulation (EU) No 596/2014** on **market abuse**—specifically the concept of **“inside information”** for **insider dealing**.

At the heart of the dispute is a familiar, high-stakes scenario: a **contracting authority decides not to award a public contract** to a tenderer. The tenderer then **sells shares early**. The question for the Court is whether that decision (and related circumstances) amounts to **inside information**—and, crucially, **what counts as “public disclosure”** of inside information by an issuer under **Article 17** of the Market Abuse Regulation (MAR), including the mechanics set out in **Implementing Regulation (EU) 2016/1055**.

**Why this matters for finance and innovation ecosystems:** procurement outcomes can move markets, especially for listed companies with defence, infrastructure, or technology contracts. If information about a contract award decision is treated as “inside information”, then **timing of trading** and **disclosure discipline** become compliance-critical. Conversely, if the information is already effectively public, the compliance burden shifts.

---

## The legal pivot: “inside information” and when it becomes public

The Court’s ruling focuses on two linked MAR concepts:

1. **Inside information criteria (Article 7(1)(a))**  
   The Court examines what makes information “inside” in the context of a **public procurement decision** not to award a contract to a tenderer. The key issue is whether such a decision is sufficiently **precise**, **non-public**, and **likely to have a significant effect** on the price of financial instruments (the standard MAR test).

2. **Public disclosure by an issuer (Article 17)** and the “means” for disclosure  
   MAR requires that when an issuer publicly discloses inside information, it must do so in a way that meets the regulation’s disclosure expectations. The case also references **Implementing Regulation (EU) 2016/1055**, which sets out **“means for public disclosure”** (notably via Article 2 of that implementing act, as flagged in the case summary).

**In plain terms:** the Court is being asked to draw a line between (a) information that is still “private” and market-moving, and (b) information that has been made public in a manner MAR recognises—so that trading restrictions no longer apply in the same way.

---

## Programme/instrument links (only what’s in the provided text)

This specific case summary does **not** mention EU innovation/defence or capital markets programmes (such as **EDF, EDIP, InvestEU, EIB/EIF, SAFE, or CMU**). So there’s nothing to connect here beyond MAR itself and its implementing regulation.

---

## Timelines, amounts, and eligibility/financing changes

- **Timeline:** judgment date **16 April 2026**; the case is **C‑229/24**.  
- **Amounts:** **no € amounts** are stated in the provided text.  
- **Eligibility/financing changes:** none are stated; this is a **legal interpretation** case, not a funding decision.

---

## Who should care: startups/SMEs, primes, and regulators

Even though the case is framed around an issuer and insider dealing, the compliance implications ripple outward.

### Startups and SMEs (especially those with listed instruments or investor-facing visibility)
- **Procurement-linked materiality is real.** If an SME is a tenderer with market exposure (e.g., listed equity, listed debt, or instruments traded on regulated venues), a **contract award decision**—or credible signals around it—can become a compliance flashpoint.
- **Trading windows and information control:** the practical lesson is to treat procurement outcomes as potentially **price-sensitive** until you can confidently map them to MAR’s “public disclosure” standards.

### Primes and contracting ecosystems
- **Information governance:** primes often sit at the centre of procurement information flows. Even if the prime isn’t the tenderer in the case, the logic can matter where information about award decisions or eligibility is shared across the supply chain.
- **Disclosure choreography:** if a prime (or a consortium member) is an issuer, it needs robust processes to ensure that any disclosure that could qualify as “public disclosure” is done in the **right form and timing**.

### Regulators and market surveillance teams
- **Consistency in “inside information” classification:** the Court’s interpretation will likely influence how national authorities assess whether procurement-related events are sufficiently precise and non-public.
- **Disclosure mechanics:** the reference to **Implementing Regulation (EU) 2016/1055** suggests regulators will scrutinise not just *what* was disclosed, but *how* it was disclosed.

**Implications for startups/SMEs, primes, and regulators**  
Even though this rulebook is aimed at **banks**, it will ripple into the ecosystem:

- **Startups and SMEs (indirect impact):**  
  Many SMEs rely on retail-facing banking channels for working capital, overdrafts, and credit lines that are ultimately administered through bank processes. Tighter conduct rules can translate into **more standardised onboarding and documentation**, which can be good news for firms that already have clean governance and reporting. The flip side is that if banks respond by tightening internal controls, some smaller borrowers may experience **slightly higher friction** (more checks, more documentation) during credit origination or servicing.

- **Primes / larger counterparties (process and compliance alignment):**  
  For primes and larger financial groups, conduct rulebooks usually mean **system and policy updates**—training, call monitoring, complaint handling, and product governance workflows. That can be operationally costly, but it also reduces regulatory uncertainty and can improve consistency across subsidiaries and distribution channels.

- **Regulators (enforcement clarity):**  
  A rulebook format tends to make supervisory expectations more concrete. That helps regulators focus on measurable compliance outcomes rather than debating interpretive boundaries. For the supervisory community, it can also improve comparability across institutions—useful when assessing conduct risk, complaint rates, and product mis-selling patterns.

**How this connects to innovation and defence (without the hype)**  
Innovation and defence financing often depends on the broader availability of credit and the stability of retail and corporate funding channels. Conduct-of-business reforms don’t directly fund R&D or defence procurement—but they can influence the **risk appetite and operational capacity** of banks that later support SME supply chains, dual-use industrial bases, and innovation ecosystems.

In other words: this is less “new money” and more **new rules of the road**. That’s not glamorous, but it’s the kind of change that quietly determines whether capital flows smoothly—or gets stuck in compliance bottlenecks.

**Two smart “so what” insights**  
1) **Conduct rules are capital-market plumbing.** Even when they target retail credit institutions, they affect how credit is originated and serviced—so they can indirectly influence the funding conditions for SMEs and the industrial base that innovation and defence rely on.
